To prevent the dissemination of potentially inaccurate results, we request the withdrawal of this paper. A revised version will be submitted once the additional work is complete</p></details> |
| **[A Survey of On-Policy Distillation for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.00626v5)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>On-Policy Distillation (OPD) trains large language models with teacher guidance on student-generated trajectories, addressing the mismatch between fixed demonstrations and the histories encountered during deployment. As its use expands across language model training, different formulations of supervision and optimization make it difficult to compare methods and understand when they are effective. This survey develops a unified view of OPD through the interplay between student-generated states, teacher information, and policy updates. This framework connects the foundations of distillation with self-distillation, multi-teacher integration, reinforcement learning, and interactive agents. We examine how these settings change the supervision available to the student and relate the resulting design choices to capability transfer, training efficiency, and deployment. Our synthesis centers on the conditions under which guidance remains reliable and learnable as the student evolves, considering both immediate performance and the capacity for subsequent learning. We conclude by discussing open challenges in adapting supervision and allocating computation to support effective learning.</p></details> | Work in Progress |
| **[AdvFidelity: Unifying Preference Alignment and Few-Step Image Generation within Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29380v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Practical image generation requires both human preference alignment and few-step efficiency, yet combining reinforcement learning (RL) with distillation is often multi-stage and may erode RL gains We observe that few-step RL progressively improves structural coherence, even with specialized rewards, but leaves low-level artifacts that these rewards may fail to penalize. To address this gap, we propose AdvFidelity, which integrates artifact-sensitive fidelity supervision into RL through an online adversarial reward. This enables joint reward alignment and high-fidelity few-step generation within a single RL stage, without distribution matching distillation (DMD) or other explicit distillation objectives. We train a discriminator to rank high-quality references (positives) above content-matched reconstructions (negatives) produced by the current generator. Reconstructing moderately noised references largely preserves style and scene layout while exposing low-level degradations. This encourages the discriminator to assess artifacts rather than content differences. With negatives reconstructed online, alternating updates adapt fidelity supervision to the generator's evolving failure modes. References can come from existing target-domain data or full-step model samples. Built on LeapAlign, AdvFidelity backpropagates gradients from both rewards through the few-step sampling trajectories used at inference. Across two model backbones and three task settings, AdvFidelity matches full-step LeapAlign in alignment, task performance, and overall generation quality and remains highly competitive with other evaluated full-step RL methods, using only 4 or 8 steps without CFG.</p></details> |  |
| **[The Answer Lies Within: Self-Derived Rewards Enable Explainable Relation Extraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.06198v4)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Despite the remarkable reasoning capabilities of large language models, they still struggle with one-shot relation extraction without predefined relation labels. We identify two pitfalls: models are often misled by irrelevant tokens instead of relation-conveying semantics, and they often fail to align with the abstraction level human annotators expect. We introduce a novel framework that closes this gap with two components: (1) COGRE, a cognitively-inspired reasoning framework that structures RE into a series of processes mimicking human text-processing; and (2) HIT@DICT, a reinforcement learning intermediate reward strategy that encourages reasoning to align with relational labels by rewarding relation-relevant phrases in reasoning. The reward is derived on a credit dictionary automatically extracted from correct predictions. Our experiments show that our framework improves both accuracy and explanation quality by addressing these two pitfalls. For example, COGRE with Qwen2.5-14B-Instruct on One-shot NYT29 achieves 24.65% F1, surpassing prior reasoning-based designs. Optimizing this approach with RL using HIT@DICT further improves performance by +23.46% points. Finally, human evaluation shows that our best model generates relational phrases closely aligned with gold labels, increasing human explanation quality ratings by 54% (relative).</p></details> |  |
| **[Beyond Policy Discrepancy: Capability-Aware Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26950v2)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-teacher on-policy distillation (MOPD) integrates domain specialists into a shared student, but determining how strongly each teacher should supervise remains challenging. Existing discrepancy-based allocation can assign strong supervision even when the student already matches or exceeds the teacher's task performance, since behavioral mismatch does not necessarily imply a remaining capability deficit. We propose Capability-Aware Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation (CA-MOPD), which combines specialist merging with capability-guided allocation to account for what the student has already learned. CA-MOPD first merges reinforcement-learning specialists into a strong student initialization, then uses verifier-estimated teacher--student capability gaps to adjust domain-level distillation strength. Supervision decreases as capability gaps close and increases when estimated student capability regresses, while dense token-level guidance remains provided by the teachers. | **[DISRL: Dual-Scale Incremental Semi-Supervised Reinforcement Learning for Dynamic Community Tracking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29315v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Dynamic local community detection tracks the evolving community of designated seed nodes across graph snapshots. However, it faces two critical challenges: temporal supervision uncertainty, where sparse and outdated labels fail to provide seed-specific guidance under topology changes, and unbounded error cascades, where sequential policy transfer accumulates exploration errors over long horizons. To address these challenges, we propose DISRL, a Dual-scale Incremental Semi-supervised Reinforcement Learning framework. DISRL introduces a supervision construction module that dynamically generates seed-specific guidance from sparse known communities, and a dual-scale community exploration module that stabilizes policy evolution by decoupling short-term adaptation within temporal windows from long-term parameter consolidation across windows. Furthermore, we construct a dynamic graph benchmark with diverse community evolution scenarios. Extensive experiments demonstrate that DISRL consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines under various dynamic settings.</p></details> |  |
| **[Flat-RLVR: Sharpness-Aware Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards for Reasoning Capacity in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29281v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR), particularly through group-relative policy optimization (GRPO), has emerged as a central paradigm for the post-training of large language models (LLMs). We theoretically link flat reward RLVR to reduced distribution sharpening, and empirically show improved sample diversity while maintaining single-attempt accuracy. Project page: https://logosroboticsgroup.github.io/TIDE/</p></details> |
| **[MA-UITD: Offline Multi-Agent Reward Inference for Characterizing Continuous Social Behavior](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29237v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-agent inverse reinforcement learning offers a powerful framework not only for imitation learning but also for understanding the neural computations supporting social interactions and decision-making. Inferring rewards from interacting agents is challenging because observed choices reflect both personal preferences and expectations about others. We introduce Multi-Agent Unilateral Inverse Temporal Difference Learning (MA-UITD), an offline method for inferring explicit agent-specific rewards from joint demonstrations in continuous-state environments with either discrete or bounded continuous actions. Its core idea is to represent the long-term consequences of each agent's own actions through belief-conditioned successor features that average over other agents' behavior. Shared reward features and agent-specific preference vectors connect these unilateral values to the target reward function, while inverse temporal-difference consistency and noise-contrastive behavioral learning enable inference without known dynamics or an inner policy-optimization loop. Across simulated cooperative, competitive, and general-sum tasks, MA-UITD outperforms baselines in discrete-action settings and infers behaviorally compatible reward profiles in continuous-action settings. When applied to experimentally collected social-foraging trajectories, MA-UITD inferred distinct spatial and interaction-dependent reward structures for leaders and followers, providing a quantitative characterization of latent behavioral objectives that may be relevant for understanding the neural computations underlying social behavior.</p></details> |  |
| **[Reasoning-Search-Augmented LLMs: An Operational Taxonomy, Benchmark Analysis and Controlled Evidence Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29189v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) demonstrate impressive emergent reasoning capabilities, yet they remain constrained by fixed model parameters and finite context windows. Even when augmented with static retrieval, they can produce statements that are unsupported by, or even contradictory to, retrieved evidence. Recent work addresses these limitations by tightly coupling step-by-step reasoning with interactive search, treating retrieval as an action within an iterative agentic loop rather than a one-shot preprocessing step. This study reviews frameworks that implement such reasoning-search loops by interleaving planning, querying, retrieval, integration, verification, and stopping. We analyze these systems through four architectural axes: retrieval coupling, controller design, knowledge source, and grounding strictness. We separately treat the learning signal as a cross-cutting dimension because prompting, supervision, preference optimization, and reinforcement learning can instantiate the same architecture while producing materially different search, stopping, and grounding behavior. We further distinguish benchmark difficulty from RSA-loop demand and synthesize controlled within-study ablations and quality-cost deltas, avoiding invalid pooling across heterogeneous model backbones, retrievers, corpora, and evaluation protocols. Our goal is to clarify the landscape of reasoning-search-augmented LLMs and outline a unified perspective on the design choices shaping future research.</p></details> | 10 pages |
| **[A Decade of Learning-Based Control: From Theoretic Foundations to Practical Deployment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29187v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Motivated by ten years of Learning-Based Control Invited Sessions at the IEEE Conference on Decision and Control (CDC), this tutorial paper reviews key advances at the intersection of machine learning and control. We focus on methods that combine the flexibility of machine learning techniques with the structure and theoretical foundations of control to design autonomous systems that are capable, reliable, and safe. We first introduce core notions for establishing stability and safety guarantees, then review approaches that learn dynamics models to improve control under uncertainty, and finally discuss data-driven methods that directly synthesize or learn policies, including reinforcement learning, imitation learning, and Bayesian optimization. We further discuss representative applications and open challenges. The goal is to provide an accessible entry point to the field and to support continued progress toward reliable learning-based control in real-world systems. To support learning and practical exploration, we share implementations and illustrative examples along with our discussion, together with additional resources on our website: https://learning-based-control.github.io/tutorial/.</p></details> |  |
| **[SAFTER: Safety-Aware Filtering Through Explicit Recoverability for Robust Reinforcement Learning under Distribution Shift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29149v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Learning safe policies that generalize to unseen environments is difficult under bounded actuation, where an unsafe decision may become unrecoverable. Its CBF condition yields an exact minimum-intervention projection without iterative online optimization. | **[Minimal Witness Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.07226v3)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>``What are the irreducible conditions that are sufficient to produce an outcome?'' is one of the most common questions that recur across computation and science. Its answers, the minimal sufficient witnesses, are what we mean by explanations, mechanisms and reasons. For base generation this default is unproblematic; under reinforcement-learning fine-tuning, however, it carries a structural limitation. The standard VAE's KL term does not merely ignore mode structure - it actively taxes separation and tightness. When data are multimodal, the ELBO optimum compresses distinct modes into an overlapping latent. RL fine-tuning amplifies this overlap: the score network is under-resolved on the saddle region between modes, dynamic-to-variational amplification rises sharply, and the collapse boundary beta_c is pushed far above its variational scale. On the real alanine-dipeptide Boltzmann distribution, a Gaussian-prior VAE collapses at beta_c = 0.224 while a mode-tracked Gaussian-mixture prior collapses at 0.00316, a 71x gap from the same pipeline. We trace this gap to a single geometric mediator, d_min/(2 sigma_0), and verify the causal chain through controlled interventions: moving geometry at fixed prior, and switching prior at matched geometry (full mediation; prior-label coefficient 0.995x, 95% CI [0.79, 1.25]). A three-source decomposition localises the amplification to the learned policy (zero ODE-solver contribution, bounded anchor mismatch); the coverage plateau is an unconditional benefit of mode-separating priors that no amount of RL capacity recovers. The remedy is conditional: a non-Gaussian prior is necessary precisely when the latent geometry would fall into overlap and the task is coverage-sensitive. A machine-checked theory skeleton supports the KL pricing structure - a divergent compactness tax under a Gaussian prior versus a finite assignment fee under a mixture - and the position-versus-plateau distinction delimits what is finite-resource from what requires changing the prior.</p></details> | <details><summary>aroun...</summary><p>around 49 pages, around 25 figures</p></details> |
| **[Self-organizing reinforcement learning: a learner-free swarm approaches the explore-exploit optimum](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25113v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The explore/exploit trade-off is the core problem of reinforcement learning (RL). We show that a minimal foraging swarm approaches its optimum - the ideal-free / Nash allocation of a multi-agent resource-sharing game (an equilibrium, not a return maximum) - with no learner and no reward signal. Agents explore by heavy-tailed (Levy) flights, recruit neighbours to a discovery through a threshold cascade on their proximity network, and disperse as a patch crowds; this cascade cycle balances exploration and exploitation and drives foragers toward the ideal free distribution. The swarm attains the shape of this optimum (committed foragers track richness with slope 0.94) but not its exact equilibrium - it stays measurably exploitable - reaching it dynamically (~150 steps) through the coupling of recruitment to movement: remove the coupling and matching collapses (0.94 -> 0.08). The RL sits at the level of the problem and the allocation reached, not the micro-rule: no step evaluates payoff (reward-free by construction, though the crowd signal is informationally tied to payoff), and the swarm reaches the allocation without learning it (quality plateaus and re-settles after resources move, no cumulative gain). Against a trained baseline the comparison is two-sided: in an easier, disembodied game a learner is more precise, while in the same embodied environment the reward-free swarm reaches ~65% of a validated oracle ceiling at zero training and leads until trained PPO and centralised-critic (CTDE) learners overtake it (~74-75%, 16 seeds, all above the swarm mean) after ~4,000 of 15,000 training steps - its advantage is in budget, not asymptote. In this one setting we read it not as beating RL but as a strong zero-shot, reward-free operating point: a demonstration that the self-organizing physics of a minimal swarm can carry a collective close to the optimum RL seeks, with none of RL's machinery.</p></details> | <details><summary>16 pa...</summary><p>16 pages, 9 figures, code and data: https://github.com/galenwilkerson/self-organizing-reinforcement-learning</p></details> |
| **[HorizonComposer: Spatiotemporally Consistent Driving Video Editing with Enriched Traffic Semantics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28829v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Instruction-guided video generative models offer a scalable solution for stress-testing autonomous driving systems by simulating diverse environmental conditions. We extract dense, pixel-level rewards from the hybrid data to serve as a localized signal and actively steer generation toward structurally reliable pixels. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our method prevents the corruption of multi-actor dynamics, establishing state-of-the-art spatiotemporal consistency, yielding consistent user preference (39% relative improvement in content preservation and 12% in temporal consistency), and achieving a significant 5% gain in downstream road line segmentation and 7% gain in downstream multi-object detection.</p></details> |  |
| **[LIFT: Scoped Constraints for Evaluating and Training Instruction Following](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28819v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Common instruction following (IF) benchmarks, such as IFEval, evaluate the ability of language models (LMs) to follow simple constraints that can easily be verified programmatically. Importantly, Lift-Bench provides both main task and instruction-following constraints, mitigating shallow reward hacking. On experiments with multiple models, we find that frontier models struggle with the harder subsets of our benchmark and analyze model performance along multiple axes. We also find that training on Lift-Train is much less susceptible to reward hacking than training on the common IFTrain. Lift-Bench and Lift-Train are publicly available, as well as the data generating pipeline.</p></details> |  |
| **[Brittle by Design? Multi-Scenario Reinforcement Learning for Robust Mission Coordination in Systems of Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28818v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As systems engineering (SE) shifts from monolithic systems to Systems of Systems, Mission Engineering (ME) has emerged as a new line of thinking in SE. Second, single-scenario coordinators can be brittle: the coordinator trained on the more forgiving scenario degrades to random tactics performance in more demanding scenarios, whereas the demanding-scenario coordinator retains most of its advantage when transferred. Third, within the same training budget, a coordinator trained on both scenarios concurrently matches the scenario-specific coordinators in their own scenarios and significantly outperforms every other policy. Sensitivity analyses on ignition location, weather, scenario, and training curricula show that the variability represented during training shapes post-deployment performance. This proof of concept shows DE-enabled mission simulations combined with RL offer a reusable methodology for improving ME practice, which can be extended to fleet design and selection from a mission-first perspective.</p></details> |  |
| **[ArcFlow: Training Flow Policies for Multiple Solver Budgets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28788v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Flow-matching policies adjust inference cost through the number of solver steps, but reinforcement learning at a single fixed solver budget can hide degradation at cheaper deployment budgets and even erase useful one-step behavior present in a pretrained policy. | **[Is Anyone Enforcing the Cartel? What Reinforcement Learning Teaches LLM Agents in Markets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28787v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Evaluations of large language model (LLM) agents report that pricing agents collude at others' expense. We introduce an audit that tests enforcement by forcing a member to cheat in counterfactual copies of a market, and use it to study what reinforcement learning (RL) teaches agents in pricing and trading. Small LLMs trained by RL learn what their training rivals reward, but only what they readily try. In self-play, they learn fixed rules ignoring their rivals; against a punishing rival, to be deterred; against a cheating rival, to match its cuts, never the costly price war that would deter it, which they rarely try. A war forced into training is learned, but mostly only where it was forced. Against their own copies, deterred and matching agents can earn the same cartel profits, but only matching ones can enforce them, and then thinly: as a by-product and only against large cuts. Q-learners enforce collusion only in pricing. Prompted LLMs reach cartel-like outcomes mostly through rules that ignore the market. Outcome measures cannot tell these cases apart; the audit can.</p></details> | <details><summary>45 pa...</summary><p>45 pages, 13 figures, 20 tables</p></details> |

| **[Path-Coupled Bellman Flows for Distributional Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08253v3)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Distributional reinforcement learning (DRL) models the full return distribution, but existing finite-support or quantile-based methods rely on projections, while recent flow-based approaches can suffer from \emph{boundary mismatch} at the flow source or from \emph{high-variance} bootstrapping when current and successor noises are independent. Across unseen target recipes, SimHack predicts the median onset to a relative error of 11.3% and achieves across-recipe rank correlation of 0.85, while generalizing to differences in model scale, curriculum, number of rollouts, and learning rate between reference and target recipes. | **[Kamino: GPU-based Simulation of Rigid Multi-Body Physics with Kinematic Loops](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.16536v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We present Kamino, a GPU-based physics solver for massively parallel simulations of heterogeneous highly-coupled mechanical systems. Implemented in Python using NVIDIA Warp and integrated into the Newton framework, it enables the application of data-driven methods, such as large-scale reinforcement learning, to complex robotic systems that exhibit strongly coupled kinematic and dynamic constraints such as kinematic loops. The latter are often circumvented by approximating the system's topology as a kinematic tree, and incorporating explicit loop-closure constraints or so-called mimic joints. Kamino aims at alleviating this burden by natively supporting these types of couplings. Moreover, Kamino supports heterogeneous worlds, allowing for batched simulation of structurally diverse robots on a single GPU. At its core lies a state-of-the-art constrained optimization algorithm based on ADMM, which computes constraint forces by solving the constrained rigid multi-body forward dynamics transcribed as a nonlinear complementarity problem. This leads to high-fidelity simulations that resolve contact dynamics without resorting to approximate models that simplify or convexify the problem. We demonstrate RL policy training on a biped with six nested kinematic loops, generating a feasible walking policy while simulating $4{,}096$ parallel environments on a single GPU.</p></details> |  |

## Robotics
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[Multimodal Embodied Agents: A Unified Capability-centric Perspective from Computer-Use to Robot-Use](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30108v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal agents (MMAs) sustain interaction through reasoning, memory, tools, and feedback, most visibly as computer-use agents, while robotic systems (RSs) couple sensing and actuation under physical dynamics. We define multimodal embodied agents (MMEAs) as goal-directed systems that couple multimodal task reasoning with physical action and revise decisions from the resulting feedback. This raises a central question: what changes when a multimodal agent moves from computer-use to robot-use? We introduce PAPAV, a unified capability-centric framework of five recurring functions: Perceive the current state, Anticipate action effects, Plan a feasible course, Act through an interface or body, and Verify the outcome. Defined by function rather than architecture, these capabilities let PAPAV identify what all three share and then compare MMEAs with MMAs and with RSs. Five physical constraints, from partial observability to unverifiable outcomes, leave fewer choices fixed in advance and less room to reverse errors. Across 64 benchmarks, explicit evaluation centers on Act; only 5 assess Anticipate and 3 assess Verify, leaving both largely hidden behind task success. These constraints also frame the open challenges we identify. PAPAV therefore provides a common basis for designing reliable physical agents and measuring progress across all five capabilities rather than task success alone.</p></details> | <details><summary>Proje...</summary><p>Project website: https://showlab.github.io/Awesome-Multimodal-Embodied-Agent/</p></details> |
| **[Implementation of Dynamic Microsegmentation for a Multi-Robot Network](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30102v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This report describes a laboratory instantiation of a dynamic microsegmentation function for an Internet-of-Things network, specifically a multi-robot network, using a commercial software-defined microsegmentation tool. Fine-grained dynamic segmentation policies are defined, where segments are adapted based on asset functional and health tags. Automated implementation of the dynamic policies in software is also undertaken. The microsegmentation solution is shown to automatically isolate an indicated threat in real time, while maintaining unaffected operations. The demonstration indicates that microsegmentation can be implemented in IoT networks, in a way that allows for robust operation in the face of threats and disruptions.</p></details> |  |
| **[AtomicRL: Atomic Reinforcement Learning for Long-Horizon Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30097v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Vision-language-action (VLA) models have shown promise in robotic manipulation, but imitation learning (IL) alone often struggles to master complex long-horizon skills. Reinforcement learning (RL) offers an effective avenue for further improving VLA policies. However, existing VLA-RL methods typically suffer from delayed and biased credit assignment due to trajectory-level sparse rewards, while imbalanced subtask mastery creates bottlenecks that limit overall performance. To address these challenges, we propose AtomicRL, an RL post-training framework that leverages atomic task structures for reliable credit assignment and adaptive exploration. AtomicRL introduces Semantic-Aware Policy Optimization (SAPO), which uses semantic-aware gating to constrain TD residuals from failed atomic subtasks from propagating across task boundaries, mitigating credit contamination while preserving intra-subtask credit assignment and value bootstrapping. In addition, Bottleneck-Adaptive Target Exploration (BATE) dynamically coordinates task sampling and local exploration incentives based on the mastery of individual atomic subtasks, directing learning toward the remaining bottlenecks. Finally, we develop a Real2Sim2Real pipeline that bridges online RL in simulation with real-world deployment, facilitating practical transfer to physical robots. On LIBERO-LONG, AtomicRL achieves absolute performance gains of 3.6% and 4.0% over PPO across two VLA architectures. On CALVIN ABC-D, it improves full-task performance by up to 9.5% over PPO. Combined with the Real2Sim2Real pipeline, AtomicRL further improves real-world task performance by an average of 21.3%. These results demonstrate that AtomicRL effectively improves the learning efficiency and manipulation performance of VLA policies on long-horizon tasks.</p></details> |  |
| **[OSCAR: Obstacle Survival Curves for Adaptive Robot Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.00990v3)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A mobile robot following a graph of known routes can make costly navigation errors when a temporary obstacle blocks a critical edge: waiting too long behind a parked cart wastes time, but immediately rerouting around a person who would move in a few seconds is also inefficient. Standard reactive obstacle avoidance addresses local motion around obstacles, while fixed wait-or-reroute rules ignore how long different obstacle types tend to persist. We propose OSCAR: an adaptive survival-modeling framework for graph-based navigation with temporary blockages. Assuming obstacle class labels are available at encounter time, the robot learns class-conditioned residual clearance-time distributions from online experience, including right-censored observations when it reroutes before observing clearance. These survival models are integrated into a time-dependent graph planner that maintains obstacle memory and computes a patience threshold at each blocked edge: how long to wait before taking an alternate route. The method continuously updates its clearance estimates across episodes and uses them to balance waiting against rerouting. We evaluate the approach in simulation and on a real mobile robot in a university atrium with obstacles including people, chairs, bins, and tubes. In simulation, the learned policy's time-to-goal converges to within 1% of an oracle with access to ground-truth clearance distributions after fewer than 20 observations per obstacle class, outperforming all heuristic baselines. Real-world deployment confirms that the policy improves online, adapting its patience thresholds from experience across 50 navigation episodes.</p></details> | <details><summary>8 pag...</summary><p>8 pages main text, appendices included</p></details> |
| **[From Reasoning to Action: Benchmarking Frontier AI Models on LIBERO-10 Robot Manipulation Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30051v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent advances in frontier AI models have demonstrated remarkable capabilities in visual understanding, reasoning, planning, and tool use, raising the possibility of using general-purpose AI models for robot manipulation without task-specific training. However, their manipulation capabilities have not been systematically evaluated on standardized robotics benchmarks. In this report, we present a unified closed-loop manipulation framework for evaluating frontier AI models on the LIBERO-10 benchmark. Our framework enables models to control a robot directly through visual observations, robot proprioception, and structured tool calls, primarily by specifying target end-effector poses and gripper commands, without task-specific fine-tuning or pretrained manipulation skills. We evaluate six frontier AI models, including GPT-6 Astra, GPT-6.1 Sol, GPT-6 Luna, Claude Opus 5, Claude Opus 5.5, and Gemini-4 Argon, across three reasoning effort levels, resulting in 18 configurations and 1,800 evaluation episodes. Our results show that GPT-6 Astra achieves the highest success rate of 44%, followed by Gemini-4 Argon at 43%. Increasing reasoning effort generally improves manipulation performance, but the benefits vary across models and come at additional inference cost. We also observe substantial differences across tasks, with models performing relatively well on object placement tasks but struggling with precise spatial arrangements and complex multi-step interactions. These findings demonstrate both the potential and limitations of frontier AI models for robot manipulation, highlighting the challenges of translating reasoning capabilities into reliable physical actions. TARSphere-Infra runs robot policies in a physics simulator with embodied NPCs and constrained dialogue. TARSphere-Harness, its embodied brain harness, lets a frozen multimodal large language model (MLLM) act through multi-level executable skills, using observations, hierarchical memory, and execution feedback to revise its decisions. On this foundation, agent-in-the-loop curation aligns task semantics, causal dependencies, and scene geometry to construct TARSphere-Bench: 360 episodes across 30 indoor scenes. Five paired conditions assess perceptual robustness, disruption adaptation, task update, human coordination, and intent understanding for navigate-and-report and fetch-and-deliver tasks. Our evaluation of 8 MLLMs shows that general-purpose models can outperform embodied models, while task updates and human interaction remain difficult. Project page: https://xuyicheng-zju.github.io/TARSphere/ Code: https://github.com/xuyicheng-zju/TARSphere</p></details> |
| **[Goal Images Are Control Targets: Spatially-Grounded Goal-Image Synthesis for Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24885v2)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Goal-conditioned manipulation policies are steered by a goal image, a picture of the desired post-manipulation scene synthesized from the current observation and a language instruction. The prevailing assumption is that better image-editing quality yields better goals, so goal generators are built and compared using general image-editing metrics. We show this assumption is false. Across two independent goal-conditioned policies on CALVIN, the image editors that top aggregate editing-quality leaderboards (Gemini 3 Pro Image and GPT Image 1.5) produce worse control targets than our proposed far smaller finetuned open models, and the gap widens sharply for the multi-step (long-horizon) tasks robots actually face. A per-dimension analysis explains why. Downstream policy success is governed almost entirely by manipulation adherence (selecting the right object and the right final state; Spearman $ρ\approx 0.9$ with success), while photorealism and aesthetic fidelity, precisely where large proprietary editors excel, are uncorrelated with success ($ρ\approx 0$). This dissociation holds on both CALVIN and the real-world-aligned SIMPLER-Bridge benchmark. We then present SGE-Goal, a spatially-grounded decomposed editing framework that predicts initial and final 2D edit-region masks before goal-image synthesis. Project page: https://riddhi-chatterjee.github.io/SGE-Goal/</p></details> |
| **[RangeNav: Task-Aligned LiDAR Range-Image Pretraining for Robot Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29845v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Autonomous mobile robots operating in dense crowds and cluttered sites require $360^\circ$ perception to react to surrounding hazards. | **[Physical Words: A Vocabulary for Structuring Latent Reasoning in Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26854v3)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal reasoning provides robotic foundation models with predictive context needed for reliable action generation. | **[Payload generalization on feedforward control actions for trajectory tracking in robots with rigid and elastic joints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29356v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Industries employ robots for repetitive tasks due to their high precision and performance, typically achieved through feedforward control actions based on an accurate robot description. Across four real-world manipulation tasks, when policies are rolled out in the world model, State Reward Model supervision increases the arithmetic mean of task-wise Pearson correlations between world-model and real-world success rates from 0.602 to 0.885. | **[ASPIRE: Agentic /Skills Discovery for Robotics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00272v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Traditional robot programming is notoriously challenging: it requires orchestrating multimodal perception, managing physical contact dynamics, and handling diverse environment configurations and execution failures. In this study, we conduct a human volunteer investigation using a 6DoF Stewart platform robot to characterize biokinematic workspace of the head-neck system and establish foundational data for human-robot interaction in precision head stabilization.</p></details> |  |
| **[Design and Control of a Multimodal Legged-Aerial Robot Through Separate Control Contraction Metrics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27315v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The integration of dynamic legged locomotion and aerial mobility offers versatility that is deterred by challenges in payload distribution, actuation, and the contrasting requirements of both domains. We search for separate domain-specific contraction metrics for walking and flight independently offline and unify them using an online energy-based Control Lyapunov Function Quadratic Program (CLF QP). This enables optimal, cross-domain control allocation, which we show using simulations. We also show experimental results that validate the mechanical viability of our hardware and the paradigm of thruster-assisted trotting. | **[RoboFake: Are Deepfake Detectors Ready for Humanoid Face Robots?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14269v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Humanoid face robots can now move their lips in sync with speech and show detailed facial expressions. We call this a RoboFake and ask whether current defenses are ready for it. We test three public face presentation attack detection (PAD) models and six public deepfake detectors on video of a physical lip-syncing face robot, on a synthesized video of the same robot, and on rendered robot faces. Thresholds are fixed on human data (BPCER 5% on FaceForensics++), and every result is compared with real human faces processed the same way as the robot videos and turned to the same head angle. A small mobile PAD model detects every physical robot frame (APCER 0%) and is not affected by compression, but its BPCER on processed human video rises to 14%; the two PAD models trained on CelebA-Spoof miss the robot in the direct capture, and one of them also misses most panel frames. Among the deepfake detectors, three flag almost every robot frame and two fewer than 7%; masking shows that the response comes from the face but does not track synthesis, and JPEG compression pushes most robot frames below the threshold for some detectors. Our results suggest that current models catch today's robots because their faces do not look human, not because they recognize the attack. Keywords: Deepfake detection, presentation attack detection, face anti-spoofing, humanoid robots, face robots, biometric security, robustness</p></details> |
| **[Visual Task Modeling and Automated Planning for Humanoid Robots in Education of Individuals with Autism Spectrum Disorder](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27162v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In robot-assisted education, a robot must understand how to complete a task in order to effectively guide a student through it. | **[CoopUQ: Robust Multi-Robot Social Navigation via Uncertainty-Aware Cooperative Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26852v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reinforcement learning enables mobile robots to make fast and scalable navigation decisions in constrained human-shared environments. | **[Sequential Planning via Anchored Robotic Keypoints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.30613v3)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Changes in a robot's observations do not automatically imply changes in the task it is solving, but language-conditioned manipulation systems often fail to separate the two. SPARK represents the task as a symbolic behavior tree whose object references are grounded to current 3D observations during execution, allowing the robot to update where actions are performed while preserving the plan. To test the separation between plan and perceptual-grounding, we perform a set of manipulation experiments with a fixed plan while moving objects during execution. Under a 10 cm displacement, verification and adaptation maintain $66.7\%$ task success, compared with $2.2\%$ when disabled, demonstrating the importance of the underlying principle. | **[LLM-Flock: LLM-Driven Decentralized Multi-Robot Flocking via Influence-Based Consensus with Collision Avoidance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.06513v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown strong capabilities in reasoning and planning, prompting researchers to explore their use as decentralized decision-makers for multi-robot formation control. | **[Embodied Playground: A Long-Horizon Agentic Robotics Benchmark for Physical, Strategic, and Spatial Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27039v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Foundation models offer a promising basis for robotic agents, but long-horizon tasks require them to sustain coherent reasoning and reliable physical execution across interdependent stages. | **[VQActFlow: Vector-Quantized Action Mode Steering for Multi-Task Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21600v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-task robot manipulation policies are challenging to learn from demonstration because traditionally a single network must select among qualitatively different action modes from a multimodal demonstration distribution, conditioned on language and visual context. | **[A Probabilistic, Rotation-Preserving Kernel Operator Surrogate for Continuum-Robot Design Spaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26605v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We develop an uncertainty-aware, operator-learning-based surrogate for tendon-driven continuum robots (TDCRs) that generalizes across robot design and actuation while predicting the complete equilibrium configuration. | **[Learning Wheel Motion Uncertainty for Degeneracy-Aware LiDAR-Inertial Odometry on Quadrupedal Wheel-Legged Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26550v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Wheel kinematics can support odometry for quadrupedal wheel-legged robots when LiDAR geometry provides weak motion constraints. | **[RIFAR: Reliability-Informed and Forgetting-Aware Replay for Continual Robot Learning with World-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03079v3)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Genuine embodied agency requires robots to turn continuous real-world experience into lasting, transferable skills. The attacker can alter the magnetic field, thus spoofing the force interpreted by magnetic tactile sensors. The attacker can stealthily deploy such an attack by simply attaching a thin (the thickness is around 0.5 mm), battery-free magnetic tag to the surface of an object. Hence, the attack tag can be easily concealed by a barcode sticker. Such an attack scheme is "deploy-and-forget" -- the attacker can deploy the tag and leave. When a robotic hand is picking up an object, it may severely damage the object. Specifically, spoofing the normal force can crush the object, whereas spoofing the shear force can lead to a loose grip, thus dropping the object. Compared with directly sabotaging the robotic hand, PhantomTac is much more concealed as the attacker does not need access to the device. | **[GemNav: Discrete-Token Visual Robot Navigation using a Multimodal Large Language Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06882v3)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Visual navigation policies built on large pretrained models increasingly rely on large cross-embodiment datasets and expressive action-generation mechanisms. Extensive offline and closed-loop experiments show improved out-of-distribution generalization over frame-based sampling and substantially more reliable obstacle navigation than a matched continuous regression head. | **[A GPU-Resident Pipeline for Multi-Robot 3D Inspection with Limited Perception](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26106v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We present a complete, GPU-resident pipeline for multi-robot 3D inspection of underwater structures. Given a triangle mesh and a fleet of $K$ robots, each carrying a short-range, limited field-of-view camera, the pipeline computes 6-DOF trajectories that jointly cover a target fraction of the surface while minimizing a linearly interpolated makespan/total-cost objective. The joint problem is NP-hard, so we decompose it into five core stages - candidate viewpoint generation, visibility computation, set cover, min-max vehicle routing solved as a mixed integer program (MIP) with lifted Miller-Tucker-Zemlin subtour elimination, and priority-based multi-agent path finding with continuous-time inter-robot conflict checking - flanked by mesh loading and surface sampling as preprocessing and by trajectory smoothing as postprocessing. Our contributions are: an end-to-end GPU-accelerated integration of the five stages, with its kernel design documented; a GPU batch adaptation of Lien's $\varepsilon$-visibility with four custom CUDA kernels; a $β$-aware per-vehicle tour upper bound derived from the interpolated objective, supplementing the Desrochers-Laporte coefficient lift with forbidden-pair cuts; a 4D space-time extension of Zhou and Zeng's GPU parallel-frontier A*; and an experimental evaluation on 14 meshes (up to $7.2$M faces) that ablates the routing cuts and the parallel-frontier search, audits collision freedom and waypoint attainment \emph{after} trajectory smoothing, and measures the end-to-end gap against analytical and LP-relaxation lower bounds. Code and raw results are publicly available.</p></details> |  |
| **[Seeing Together: Multi-Robot Cooperative Egocentric Spatial Reasoning with Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.18431v3)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have made substantial progress in egocentric video understanding, but their ability to reason cooperatively from multiple embodied viewpoints remains largely unexplored. We study this problem through multi-robot cooperative dynamic spatial reasoning, where a model must answer spatial, temporal, visibility, and coordination questions by integrating synchronized egocentric videos from a team of moving robots. To support this setting, we introduce CoopSR, the first benchmark for this task, together with EgoTeam, a multi-robot egocentric QA dataset. EgoTeam contains 114,227 QA pairs spanning 19 question types, four difficulty tiers, and three team sizes in Habitat and iGibson, along with a real-world test set of around 2,326 QAs collected using two quadruped robots. We further propose SG-CoR (Spectral and Geometry-Aware Cooperative Reasoner), an MLLM framework for fine-grained cooperative spatial reasoning. SG-CoR combines dynamics-aware multi-robot frame sampling, spectral- and geometric-guided view fusion, and geometry- and motion-informed prompt distillation, enabling the model to benefit from privileged robot-pose supervision during training while requiring only egocentric videos at test time. Across 22 MLLM baselines, SG-CoR consistently improves cooperative reasoning, outperforming the strongest fine-tuned baseline by +3.87% on Habitat and +7.12% on iGibson. It also shows stronger generalization to unseen team sizes and real-world robot tests. Code can be found at https://github.com/KPeng9510/seeing-together.git.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by NeurIPS 2026</p></details> |
| **[A Spiking Ring-Attractor Architecture for Representation and Control of Revolute Robot Joints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26027v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Robotic systems with revolute joints evolve on circular state spaces, yet most control architectures represent these states using Euclidean variables that ignore this underlying geometry. We propose a fully spiking neural control architecture in which ring attractors encode joint states, enforcing representations consistent with the topology of revolute systems. Homeostatic regulation computes goal-state mismatch directly over these distributed representations, and opponent gain-modulated populations convert this mismatch into controlled phase shifts of the activity bump, implementing set-point regulation as motion on the ring. We show that the bump dynamics follow an SO(2) geometry, and that gain asymmetries induce a signed phase velocity on the ring. To interface distributed activity with scalar regulation signals, we derive an analytic Fourier phase decoder and implement its signed components using push-pull spiking populations. For multi-joint systems, we demonstrate that local ring-based Fourier features compose into product representations that linearly decode SO(3) orientations through forward kinematics. Experiments in NEST validate the full SO(2) regulation mechanism and the spiking product-feature decoder for lift, pitch, and yaw. Robot experiments on a Tiago arm show that decoded ring dynamics generate joint velocity commands, producing directed motion with joint-dependent accuracy. Together, these results establish a neuromorphic control framework in which representation, regulation, and action are unified within a ring-attractor substrate aligned with the geometry of revolute systems.</p></details> | 23 pages, 10 figures |
| **[SkateNav: Learning Energy-Efficient Navigation through Adaptive Skating on Passive-Wheel Quadrupedal Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25962v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Passive-wheel quadrupedal robots offer energy-efficient locomotion through leg-driven propulsion and momentum-driven gliding. However, navigation requires coordinating steering with intermittent propulsion and adapting push-glide timing to task demands. Velocity-tracking objectives may suppress the speed variations inherent in skating, discouraging efficient gliding. To address these challenges, we propose SkateNav, a unified skating-navigation framework based on reinforcement learning. A time-budgeted goal-reaching objective allows the policy to jointly learn steering, propulsion, and their temporal allocation. An adaptive skating cycle structures gliding, pushing, and recovery, while frequency modulation at every control step adjusts phase durations to navigation demands. Simulation experiments demonstrate higher average success rates and lower cost of transport than the evaluated planner-controller baselines, with ablations confirming the contributions of cycle structure and frequency adaptation. Real-world experiments demonstrate zero-shot transfer from simulation to a passive-wheel Mini Cheetah across cluttered indoor spaces, slopes, and paved outdoor surfaces. Supplementary videos are available on the project page: https://ktw1404.github.io/SkateNav/</p></details> | <details><summary>8 pag...</summary><p>8 pages, 12 figures, 3 tables. Project page: https://ktw1404.github.io/SkateNav/</p></details> |
| **[RoboPocket: Improve Robot Policies Instantly with Your Phone](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.05504v3)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Scaling robot learning is bottlenecked not only by data volume, but also by whether the data targets the policy's failures. Existing robot-free handheld interfaces enable portable in-the-wild data collection, yet remain open-loop: users record demonstrations without feedback from the current policy. Interactive methods such as DAgger provide such feedback by collecting corrective on-policy data, but require deploying imperfect policies on physical robots, making them costly, unsafe, and difficult to scale. We present RoboPocket, a smartphone-based system for robot-free policy iteration. By combining on-device sensing, display, and communication with cloud-side policy inference, RoboPocket provides real-time collection guidance and visualizes the policy's predicted trajectory in augmented reality as AR Visual Foresight. This allows users to identify policy weaknesses and collect targeted corrective data without deploying a physical robot. An asynchronous finetuning pipeline continuously incorporates new corrections and updates the policy within minutes, realizing a DAgger-inspired robot-free correction loop. Across diverse real-world manipulation tasks, we validate RoboPocket as a scalable data engine and show that online corrective data collection improves sample efficiency by up to $2\times$ over scaling offline demonstrations. Project page and videos: https://robo-pocket.github.io.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to CoRL 2026. Project page: https://robo-pocket.github.io</p></details> |
| **[Vision-Language-Delta-Action: Learning Compact Deltas as Actionable Transition Interfaces for Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25874v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Robotic manipulation is not merely observation-to-action generation, but the realization of task-specified state changes in the physical world. Existing vision-language-action (VLA) methods directly learn observation-to-action mappings, imposing a substantial learning burden due to the gap between semantic understanding and action execution. Although world-action models (WAMs) improve control through future prediction, modeling complete future states wastes capacity on static appearance and action-irrelevant changes. We propose Vision-Language-Delta-Action (VLDA), a paradigm that reformulates full future prediction as compact actionable transition modeling. Its core is a compact Delta variable that captures latent world-state changes, serving as an interface between vision-language understanding and action generation. VLDA learns Delta through a unified objective composed of the Local Transition Filter (LT-Filter) and Global Transition Anchor (GT-Anchor). LT-Filter captures task-relevant manipulation-centric changes while suppressing static reconstruction biases. GT-Anchor provides coarse future-level regularization to preserve the overall direction of state changes. Delta-Driven Action Refinement (DDAR) further grounds Delta in the action manifold and injects it as a structured seed to guide action denoising. We implement VLDA within LoongForge, an open-source training framework for large-scale foundation and embodied models, using its modular and distributed infrastructure for unified two-stage optimization. Experiments show that VLDA improves long-horizon consistency, robustness, and generalization, validating actionable Delta as an alternative to direct action prediction and full future-state modeling.</p></details> | <details><summary>37 pa...</summary><p>37 pages, including appendix. Code framework: https://github.com/baidu-baige/LoongForge/tree/master/cookbook/loongforge-vlda</p></details> |
| **[TrajWAM: An Efficient Trajectory-Guided World Action Model for Dynamic Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25763v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Dynamic object manipulation requires robots to anticipate target motion while replanning fast enough to avoid executing outdated actions. Existing approaches either rely on fast reactive control without an explicit future target state or encode anticipated motion only implicitly. World Action Models (WAMs) offer a promising alternative by coupling future visual prediction with action generation, but scene-level video objectives may underrepresent target dynamics that are critical for manipulation, while iterative video-action denoising introduces substantial latency. We present TrajWAM, a trajectory-guided world action model for efficient dynamic robotic manipulation. Our key insight is to explicitly ground future visual imagination in predicted target-object motion and use the resulting future representation to condition action generation, while preserving this anticipation pathway after acceleration. Given a short history of object masks, a history-conditioned motion predictor forecasts future masks encoding the target object's trajectory. A lightweight Trajectory ControlNet then steers a pretrained video model toward trajectory-consistent futures. The resulting future representation conditions an action expert to predict executable action chunks. To enable one-step inference without breaking this interface, we introduce a decoupled distillation scheme that combines distribution matching for the video backbone, Trajectory ControlNet realignment, and action rollout distillation to support accurate one-step action prediction conditioned on the compressed future representation. Comprehensive evaluations in simulation and the real world show that TrajWAM achieves state-of-the-art performance on dynamic object manipulation tasks while enabling efficient one-step video-action inference. Project page: https://kevingeng3.github.io/TrajWAM/.</p></details> |  |
| **[DeReCo: Decoupling Representation and Coordination Learning for Object-Adaptive Decentralized Multi-Robot Cooperative Grasping and Lifting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.08111v3)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Generalizing decentralized multi-robot cooperative grasping and lifting across objects with diverse shapes and physical properties remains a fundamental challenge. Under decentralized execution, two key challenges arise: object-dependent representation learning under partial observability and coordination learning in multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) under non-stationarity. A typical approach jointly optimizes object-dependent representations and coordinated policies in an end-to-end manner while randomizing object shapes and physical properties during training. However, this joint optimization tightly couples representation and coordination learning, which can introduce bidirectional interference: inaccurate representations under partial observability can degrade coordination learning, while non-stationarity in MARL can further degrade representation learning, leading to degraded task performance. Inspired by prior work on decoupling representation and control learning in single-robot settings, we propose DeReCo, a MARL framework that decouples representation and coordination learning for object-adaptive decentralized multi-robot cooperative grasping and lifting. DeReCo adopts a two-phase training strategy: (1) coordination learning with privileged object information, and (2) transition to decentralized execution through reconstruction of object-dependent representations from local observations and retraining of coordination policies using the reconstructed representations. Experimental results show that DeReCo outperforms baselines in simulation on three training objects, generalizes to six unseen objects with varying masses and friction coefficients under similar grasping configurations, and achieves superior performance on two unseen objects in real-robot experiments.</p></details> | 9 pages, 7 figures |
| **[Validation of Human Motor Development Scales with Humanoid Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25701v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We investigate how humanoid robots can learn a series of motor skills defined by human infant motor development scales, and we evaluate which scales are comprehensive enough to serve as self-contained guides for developing gross motor skills up to standing and walking. We represent each motor development scale as a developmental graph that guides the training of a humanoid robot. We then apply this graph within a reinforcement learning framework so that the robot learns skills following the natural phases of human motor development described by the scale. Our work demonstrates that graphs derived from three out of four popular pediatric motor scales can successfully guide humanoid robots to develop these skills. The fourth scale misses some intermediate milestones that the other scales have, which causes some failures. After adding a missing milestone in its graph, it also enables the robot to develop those skills successfully.</p></details> | <details><summary>8 pag...</summary><p>8 pages, 7 figures. Submitted to ICRA 2027. Video available at https://youtu.be/SR3LUVhohV4</p></details> |
| **[CoEvolve: Self-Evolving Code Policies from Skills to Strategies for Humanoid Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23288v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent advances in Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models and World Action Models (WAMs) have expanded the manipulation and whole body capabilities of individual robots. Yet a single robot remains limited by its embodiment, and many real world tasks require capabilities that can only emerge through cooperation. Existing multi robot frameworks largely emphasize task decomposition, allocation, and replanning, while providing limited support for adapting executable coordination when physical execution deviates from the intended behavior. We present CoEvolve, a framework that represents team coordination as executable code over fixed robot motor skills. CoEvolve introduces Verifier Guided Coordination Evolution, where a multimodal foundation model instantiates coordination strategies through motion primitives, semantic objectives, temporary robot roles, and bounded execution parameters. Physical verification evaluates each executed transition, rejected behaviors trigger structured coordination revision from an accepted checkpoint, and verified experience is retained to guide subsequent decisions. We evaluate CoEvolve with two simulated Unitree G1 humanoids transporting a freely supported rigid payload through constrained corridors. Across 15 base task runs with three multimodal backbone families, CoEvolve completes 8 tasks, with every backbone achieving end to end success. These results support executable coordination as a practical interface for adapting long horizon multi robot collaboration under shared physical constraints.</p></details> |  |
| **[CusADi2: Latency-Optimized Batch Computation of Robot Kinematics, Jacobians, and Hessians on the GPU](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25476v1)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Trajectory optimization methods, from sampling-based zero-order to gradient-based first- and second-order approaches, require many evaluations of dynamical system functions, i.e., functions derived from a robot's dynamical model, such as forward kinematics and their Jacobians and Hessians. These evaluations are largely independent, e.g., across sampled trajectories or time steps, and can thus be evaluated in parallel as a batch. When used to make decisions online, these methods require low latency, i.e., each batch must complete in as little wall-clock time as possible. Existing frameworks utilize modern GPU hardware primarily to parallelize across batch instances, thereby optimizing throughput but not latency. We present CusADi2, a software library that reduces the batch evaluation latency by additionally parallelizing computation within each batch instance. We benchmark forward kinematics, Jacobians, and Hessians for all 67~robots in the MuJoCo Menagerie, including arms, hands, quadrupeds, humanoids, and biomechanical models, against MuJoCo XLA, MuJoCo Warp, frax, CusADi, and CasADi. CusADi2 achieves lower latency than all baselines at batch sizes of 64 to 4096, while matching them at larger batch sizes. We also demonstrate the performance advantage of CusADi2 in a sampling-based control application. All code is open-source at: https://github.com/johnviljoen/cusadi2</p></details> | <details><summary>8 pag...</summary><p>8 pages, 4 figures. Submitted to IEEE Robotics and Automation Letters (RA-L). Code: https://github.com/johnviljoen/cusadi2</p></details> |
| **[SurGPT: A Multi-Task Foundation Model for Robotic Surgical Skills Assessment and Gesture Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25426v1)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The assessment of surgical skills in robotic surgery training has traditionally relied on subjective evaluations by human experts, creating bottlenecks in surgical education and limiting scalability. We present a novel foundation model that automatically analyzes robotic surgical videos to provide comprehensive skill assessment and gesture understanding. Combining a visual encoder with a transformer architecture, the model performs three key tasks simultaneously: 1) surgical task classification (knot tying, needle passing, suturing), 2) skill level assessment on a standardized scale, and 3) natural language generation of surgical gesture descriptions. The model processes video sequences from the da Vinci surgical robot and generates human-readable text descriptions of surgical actions, similar to how language models generate text from prompts, using a multi-task learning framework with adaptive uncertainty weighting to optimize performance across all objectives. Our transformer-based architecture incorporates positional encoding for temporal understanding and attention mechanisms for spatiotemporal feature integration. Evaluated on the da Vinci Skills Simulator dataset, the proposed framework demonstrates state-of-the-art performance in surgical task recognition and provides interpretable skill assessments that correlate with expert evaluations, with the model's ability to generate natural language descriptions of surgical gestures opening new possibilities for automated surgical education, real-time feedback systems, and objective skill assessment in surgical training and certification programs.</p></details> |  |
| **[Learning When to Adapt: Reactivity-Conditioned Residual Adaptation under Human-Robot Distribution Shift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25419v1)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Pedestrians differ in whether they respond to a robot, yet many RL policies assume all are non-reactive to prioritize safety. A Transformer-based estimator infers latent pedestrian reactivity from motion histories and robot context, providing a more accurate signal for navigation adaptation and improving scenario-averaged simulation accuracy by $12.62$ percentage points over the Bayesian baseline, which assumes known pedestrian goals. These estimates are spatially aggregated into an adaptation scale that gates LoRA modules, enabling cooperative behavior near reactive pedestrians and conservative behavior near non-reactive pedestrians. Among baselines achieving SR above $90\%$ in every scenario, RCRA reduces scenario-averaged path length by $16.7\%$ relative to the conservative backbone and by $7.3\%$ relative to randomized-training baselines. It improves mean SR by $14.88$ percentage points over the reactive-trained policy.</p></details> |  |
| **[MVISTA-4D: View-Consistent 4D World Model with Test-Time Action Inference for Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.09878v3)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>World-model-based imagine-then-act becomes a promising paradigm for robotic manipulation, yet existing approaches typically support either purely image-based forecasting or reasoning over partial 3D geometry, limiting their ability to predict complete 4D scene dynamics. To efficiently learn the multi-view, cross-modality generation, we explicitly design cross-view and cross-modality feature fusion that jointly encourage consistency between RGB and depth and enforce geometric alignment across views. Beyond prediction, converting generated futures into actions is often handled by inverse dynamics, which is ill-posed because multiple actions can explain the same transition. We address this with a test-time action optimization strategy that backpropagates through the generative model to infer a trajectory-level latent best matching the predicted future, and a residual inverse dynamics model that turns this trajectory prior into accurate executable actions. Experiments on three datasets demonstrate strong performance on both 4D scene generation and downstream manipulation, and ablations provide practical insights into the key design choices.</p></details> |  |
